#6789. I Am the Wildest Dream of Every Black and Brown Trans Woman Before Me

And it is my duty to honor their legacy while making a way for the next generation of trans girls, just like our luminaries did for me.

As an influencer, a lot of times I'm the only trans person—or the only trans person of color—in the room. It’s a lot of pressure, and there have been times where I have anxiety before going to certain spaces because I will question "Do I belong here?" But I’ve learned that that’s just the patriarchy and society trying to make us feel like we're not worthy.

That’s especially true in this current political and cultural climate. The trans community, especially the Black and brown trans community, gets so much hate—that’s why I’m very offended when people say being trans “is a choice.” Like, living with and navigating these stigmas and mistreatments isn't something that someone would willing choose.

It’s been particularly challenging, too, as a trans person in the public eye; there's been many instances where my safety has been jeopardized. When I get looked at, I'm thinking, They're looking at me either because I'm pretty or because I'm a viral trans person online. The differentiator is literally if I'm being chased home, which has happened multiple times. Since I speak out for trans women that can't speak for themselves, I'm also putting myself at risk. But at the same time, I think I can also make it safer for the next girl down the line.

It's so rewarding being that person others can look up to. I love reassuring other trans women that they can do certain things or just live their lives authentically, loud, and open. But sometimes I'm like, Oh my god, how nice and easy would it be for me to just be silent and be in the background? But I don't think I was born to be a background girl. I'm front and center.

And I think that the trans women before me would want me to be. The hard work that those women put for girls like me now, I have to show up for that. I think about trans women on the front lines of trans rights, like Marsha P. Johnson or Sylvia Rivera—they were front and center at Stonewall. They were the voices for many then, and there was so much more violence. The community was pretty much hidden at the time, too.

There's so much trans history lost, and Trans Day of Remembrance resurfaces those stories. A lot of times things get pushed under the rug because trans people are seen as not important to the media, or people in general, especially after all the propaganda that's been pushed out by certain administrations. But every year on this day, I learn about a different girl, especially outside of the U.S. I’ve learned so much about how some surgeries started because certain trans women needed gender-affirming care, and they paved the way for girls down the line.

Education is the number one thing. I want people to know that your understanding of trans identities is not what you think and that there's a lot more to learn. There are times when I get frustrated because I see the stigmas and the rhetoric online that's really just misinformation, rumors, and lies that people make up about trans people. A lot of trans women have died because that ignorance can lead to trans violence. It's really sad to see. But we made it back then—and it was hard then—so we'll make it now. We're a strong community.

The trans community, we show up for ours (for the most part). There's a couple spaces made for Black trans queer people, like the Black Trans Kitchen by Queen Jean—it’s really beautiful. And Gia Love created the annual Celebration of Black Trans Women Cookout. Trans women that make spaces like that really inspire me because they remind our community that we matter. And we as trans people need spaces where we can just be ourselves, and really show care to one another. Those spaces not only keep me humble, but also remind me of everything I've been through. It makes me feel so grateful and privileged to be alive today because a lot of girls don't make it.

With the work that I do and everything I create, I want other trans women to see me and know that they belong and that they can be in those spaces too. I want to see more, especially more trans women of color, in positions of power and in rooms where you rarely ever see them.

I hope other trans women see that they can be their most authentic self, and that they can feel confident regardless of what anybody says. If you believe you're an amazing, beautiful person, it will reflect on the outside. I want people to feel that way and do that inner work because it's important, especially for our survival as trans people.

#6787. From the Archive: Ina Garten Tapped a Few Famous Friends for Her '90s Farmhouse Renovation

Take a look inside the sunny, five-room farmhouse.

Welcome to Archive Dive—my new excuse to obsess over the absolute treasures hiding in House Beautiful’s 130-year-old archives. Every month, we’ll dig up a favorite story from the past (epic room makeovers, wildly bold design swings, and style moments that were way ahead of their time) and talk about why they still resonate today. It’s part history lesson, part design deep-dive, and 100 percent a love letter to the iconic taste that’s defined HB for over a century.—Jo Saltz, House Beautiful Editor in Chief

After Ina Garten moved the now-shuttered Barefoot Contessa specialty store from Westhampton to East Hampton, she and her husband, Jeffrey, decided to put down roots in the neighborhood. But rather than buy a new-build home, Garten transformed an old farmhouse into a light-filled, five-room retreat designed for hosting—and feeding—guests.

In our latest Archive Dive, we revisit the 1994 renovation, which includes Garten’s search for the right paint color, her no-nonsense decorating approach, and her love of the property’s grounds. She took each project in stride with help from famous friends like Martha Stewart and the late interior designer Robert Currie—and Jeffrey, of course. The result was a relaxed home with an entertaining-ready atmosphere and a garden that would make any green-thumbed foodie proud. Read on for the original story.

With the help of Robert Currie, the owner of a fabulous food emporium in the Hamptons planned her nearby house for the casual ease of the legions of guests she feeds in her spare time.

Are you full of self-doubt in the dark of the night? Should you buy that old house? Should you change your career? Then follow in Ina Garten’s (bare) footsteps. She has done both with such breathtaking speed and success that the average worrier blinks in awe. She bought a Westhampton, New York, specialty store (oddly shaped breads, ravishing vegetables, seafood salad to go) from a business opportunities ad practically on the spot.

“I didn’t know how to slice smoked salmon or tell whether the Brie was ripe,” she confesses. But in no time, she turned her emporium, Barefoot Contessa, into a foodie’s fairyland.

After Garten moved the store to East Hampton she converted a cramped, run-down old farmhouse in the village into an airy five-room refuge and party venue for herself and her husband. “I just trusted that if the house had good ‘bones,’ I could figure it all out,” she laughs. No insomnia. No agonies. And, incredibly, no mistakes.

She’s a nurturer, too, a definite Earth Mother, which is certainly why she fled her career in nuclear energy policy planning for the far more congenial field of food. Her personal policy is summed up simply: lots of entertaining.

When her husband—a former Columbia professor, author, and investment banker—worked in New York, she encouraged his uptight fellow bankers to loosen their ties and shed their jackets by dishing up platters of fried chicken and other finger foods. In East Hampton she gives Sunday afternoon parties with plenty of easy food “like crabcake sandwiches,” and leaves all the doors open so guests will feel free to wander out and eat on the porch, or anywhere else that seems inviting.

She often serves in her sunny kitchen, which contains the requisite serious fridge and stove, but lacks the usual array of self-important utensils. This is a kitchen with nothing to prove.

The toughest decisions of her fix-up were the wall colors. She thought at first that she might like the whole house vanilla (a true foodie’s choice), but test patches were too white or yellow.

“I had samples all over the walls, and the painter was yelling ‘Stop already’,” but finally she hit on a toasty khaki for her bedroom. She was still stuck for the first floor when she spotted the bedroom primer paint: khaki halved with white. A mental lightbulb turned on, and the painter went to work. The house is now two values of that changeable shade with browny greens in the upholstery derived from the tapestry-covered living room sofa.

There is not a stick of unnecessary furniture in the farmhouse either, just several old oversize tables, a few pieces of portly upholstery, and in the bedroom, a brass bed (of which a friend said, “That’s not a bed, it’s a temple”), plus two chairs. No windows are curtained.

“Everything,” Garten feels, “should do something, have a function, not just sit there. I like things to be spare.” And bare as well, like her new floors, which are crafted of newly purchased old wood. Are the original floors still underneath? Nope. With a sanding and a coat of polyurethane, they are reincarnated as a kitchen countertop.

Garten actually bought the house for its grounds, because she yearned to garden. She had lived for a time in rented houses, but when she found herself planting expensive trees in rented lawns, she realized it was time to own some land.

Outside her kitchen doors she has cut square beds for tomatoes, which mix quite cheerfully with dahlias, and for roses knee-deep in catmint. She likes to combine “mess with structure,” but her structures are always imaginative. Instead of a single stake for each tomato plant, for instance, she does a row of tepees. Or she lines her disheveled strawberry beds with a spine of iris.

A Manhattan florist friend, Tom Pritchard of Pure Mädderlake, helped her with the garden design and did a very clever thing. He took two chairs out into the garden and walked around with her, scoping out the best seating spots. That’s how she decided which vistas to emphasize and which had too fine a view of the neighbor’s patio. Gardening has become a passion since she moved here. Every surface in her house is piled with well-thumbed horticultural tomes; for the time being, her food banks are relegated to the bottom of the pile.

Garten acknowledges that she has had a lot of help. Her good friend, the late Robert Currie, designed all the seating, shopped with her, and arranged the furniture. He also taught her to avoid like mad “anything that was too trendy.” Stephen Scanniello, the rose dean at the Brooklyn Botanic Garden, chose a hundred great roses for her, and Martha Stewart passed judgment on her new shower stall. No less helpful was her very supportive husband.

But husbands don’t ponder that midnight ceiling with you. And talented friends aren’t around at 2 a.m., when the real bullets are bitten. Some lucky people, though, do it all with ease—Ina Garten for one. Warm and comfy, like her own new house, she just turns over and goes back to sleep.

#6785. Cecilie Bahnsen Rethinks the Fleece for Her Second Uniqlo Collaboration

The collection is a Scandi winter balletcore dream.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Cecilie Bahnsen has a cult following for a reason: the Danish designer’s big blooming dresses and dedication to sporty, easy balletcore have been collected in droves by fashion far and wide. So when Uniqlo came calling for her to design a second collaboration, it was time again for the designer to take the reins and merge her playful girlish Scandi style with the utilitarian wearability of the Japanese giant. The collection, which includes womenswear and childrenswear, puts a focus on cozy winter wardrobe dressing and is available now. In it, Bahnsen is rethinking and retooling the classic winter fleece.

For the occasion, Bahnsen spent half a year designing the collection and also collaborated with her friend Fumiko Imano, the artist, to shoot the campaign in Sweden amidst a picturesque landscape. “It was very much about sisterhood, the journey you do of exploring together, and inspiration also from when I’m in the countryside with my son, and we collect flowers, and how we kind of, he sometimes pinned them on his jumper,” Bahnsen tells ELLE of the new collection. “I think it was really kind of that romantic or nostalgic feeling that I wanted to put into the collection. And then there was also this notion of just working with one fabrication and working with the fleece for winter and how you can work this as layering.”

While Bahnsen in particular is known for her flouncy babydoll dresses, when it came to her second collection for Uniqlo, she tells us it was all about “refinement and balance and creating beautiful kind of everyday pieces.” Her personal favorite piece of the lineup is the fleece in the palest shade of soft pastel pink, embroidered with small flowers. “For me, the fleece is such a classic layering piece,” she says. She’s already been wearing it herself for countryside outings. “There’s romance and nostalgia to pink, and I think with this collaboration, it was nice to do the fleece in pink,” she says. “Showing that it could have that femininity to it was special, and it is also that right tone as a super soft pink that I love.”

The entire collection is designed to be layered, and the fleece is at the center of it. There’s a cropped fleece vest, a sheer long T-shirt printed with subtle bouquets of flowers, a floral-embellished fleece skirt and matching socks, gloves, and hats. Bahnsen herself recommends taking a cue from her own runway shows. “I always love a big jumper or a big fleece over a poof-y dress,” she says. “It has both a super boyish vibe to it but also a princess-y, romantic feeling, too, so I love those kinds of contrasts between each other.”

“These are pieces that you want to not just to work for one season, but to be timeless; it’s become about the perfection of these small details that makes it unique and maybe more romantic than what is normally in the Uniqlo collection,” adds Bahnsen. “I feel like the palette has a really Scandinavian winter tone to it, so how do you balance this soft pink into the darker blacks and blues, and kind of build it up, not in a monocomatic way, but really putting the tones and the layering together.” It’s definitely a chicer way to stay warm. The designer’s latest is the ultimate study of form and function.

#6781. Jennifer Lawrence Joins the Ranks of Celebrities Talking About Their Breast Implants

The Hunger Games actress opened up about her procedure and her surgeon on Watch What Happens Live

Jennifer Lawrence has always leaned toward honesty in interviews, earning her a reputation as one of Hollywood’s most relatable celebrities. And as online chatter about plastic surgery—who did it, and what did they do—reaches a fever pitch, Lawrence is setting the record straight by openly discussing her “new” breast implants.

During an appearance on Watch What Happens Live with Andy Cohen, alongside her Hunger Games co-star Josh Hutcherson, Lawrence told Cohen offhandedly that she “has implants.” When Cohen asked, she said they’re new and credited Santa Monica–based plastic surgeon Steven Teitelbaum.

Lawrence went on to say she went with a full-C implant, restoring her breasts to the “same size they were”—meaning, before she had two kids. This is the second time in recent months that the Oscar winner has admitted to a postpartum plastic surgery procedure. In an interview with The Cut for her new haircare brand, Mes, Lawrence admitted to getting a breast lift following the birth of her second child. “Everything bounced back, pretty much, after the first one,” the actress told The New Yorker in October 2025. “Second one, nothing bounced back.”

She took that sentiment even further on an October 2026 episode of Call Her Daddy, where she gave host Alex Cooper, who was then pregnant, a frank piece of motherly wisdom: “Your vagina will bounce back, your butthole won’t.”

Lawrence is the latest in a string of A-list celebrities opening up about cosmetic treatments, like Kylie Jenner, who made waves last year when she admitted on TikTok to her exact implant size and surgeon. From Lawrence, the casual admission feels less like a calculated confession and more like advice from a trusted friend who doesn’t hold back about motherhood and pregnancy’s physical toll.

#6780. Azzedine Alaïa Changed Fashion on His Own Terms

A new exhibition showcases 80 garments from the rule-breaking designer.

In the 1980s, Azzedine Alaïa acquired a title that followed him for the rest of his career: the “King of Cling.” The moniker was a fitting one: the Tunisian couturier was renowned for his sculptural, body-centric designs. He experimented with stretch jersey and “anti-stress” fibers to sculpt the body without restricting movement.

But the nickname was also limiting, failing to capture the full scope of his work. The first major U.S. retrospective devoted to Alaïa, “Azzedine Alaïa: Sculpting Fashion,” at San Francisco’s Legion of Honor, brings his broader legacy into focus through 80 garments and selections from his personal collection of historic fashion.

“As my research went on, I came to realize how important the U.S. market was in Alaïa’s launch,” Laura L. Camerlengo, co-curator of the exhibit and curator in charge of Costume and Textile Arts at the Fine Arts Museums of San Francisco, tells ELLE. “By the [brand’s] third season, the U.S. was the second highest market after France, [with interest from] both luxury department stores and small independent retailers.”

The exhibition took shape after Camerlengo reached out to Olivier Saillard, director of the Fondation Azzedine Alaïa in Paris, who became her co-curator. As visitors enter the Legion of Honor’s Rosekrans Court, they encounter a dress designed for Grace Jones: a spring/summer 1986 haute couture look that Jones modeled on the runway and wore to the 1985 Oscars de la Mode. Part of the opening display, titled “The Women Who Made Alaïa,” it sets the tone for a show that recognizes the muses—both famous and private clients—who wore and supported his work.

Also on view are looks made famous by Rihanna—namely, a sheer red halter gown with a flowing train that she wore to the 2013 Grammys—and Tina Turner, whose silk-organza dress embroidered with gold-tone beads from Alaïa’s spring/summer 1989 concludes the exhibition.

The show then turns to Alaïa’s iconography, as Camerlengo puts it: the bandage dress, his work in denim, and zippered dresses, as well as rare, lesser-seen gems. “I was really keen to have early works on view, particularly the pieces from the 1980s, when he was launching his brand,” the curator says. “These are pieces that would have been shown at Bergdorf Goodman when he had his first fashion show in the United States.”

The exhibition also places Alaïa’s designs in conversation with the museum’s permanent collection. Belle Epoque-style ensembles from his spring/summer 1992 collection appear among 18th-century European paintings and furnishings, including a Canaletto view of Venice and a sofa commissioned for Marie Antoinette.

Alaïa’s own collection of historic fashion, previously shown in the 2023 Paris exhibition “Azzedine Alaïa, couturier collectionneur,” is represented here, too. The gallery includes pieces by fashion houses he admired, such as Lanvin, Chanel, Balenciaga, and Schiaparelli.

Though Alaïa, who died in 2017 at age 82, is remembered for the body-conscious designs that earned him that “King of Cling” title, he was also a renegade in an industry built around constant production. He showed collections only when he felt they were ready—a maverick approach that still resonates today—and had his creations worn by a who’s who of the modeling world, from Naomi Campbell to Gisele Bündchen. Many of his designs were featured in the pages of ELLE over the decades.

“He was someone who pioneered a new way of thinking about fashion,” Camerlengo says. “He was one of the first to question the fashion system and seasonal calendars and the need for trends. He was focused on meticulous craftsmanship and intentionality in design, a sort of precedent to what we think about today, where we are critical of overproduction and overconsumption in fashion.”

“Azzedine Alaïa: Sculpting Fashion” is on view at the Legion of Honor Museum from Nov. 21, 2026, to Apr. 11, 2027.

#6778. The Couple Created a Giant Sunflower Walkway For Their Multicultural Sun Valley Wedding

Parker Selman and Karan Rai’s love story was born from a sunset. “Our paths crossed a few times before, but the first time we remember speaking was on a roof as we watched the sun go down our senior year at Tufts University,” Parker recalls. A few weeks later, they found themselves alone on a dance floor. “I looked down at my phone to see it was midnight and, therefore, officially my birthday,” Parker says. “I told him he should give me a kiss because it was my birthday, and he did. We were both smitten, and constantly in one another's orbit.” Despite the two lining up jobs on opposite coasts, they quickly realized being practical was not an option, and Karan managed to convince his company to transfer him to New York.

The couple got engaged on a crisp autumn walk around Governors Island—Parker’s favorite spot in the city. She owns and operates a wedding photography business, and as they strolled along, she asked to take a picture of Karan on her medium-format camera with their apartment building and skyline in the background. “While I was snapping the photo, he got down on one knee,” she says, “and I had the privilege of capturing my own proposal.” They rode the ferry back to Manhattan and went to Della’s, a local wine bar, where their families and friends were waiting to celebrate.

Two months later, she proposed back. “While we were walking in Sun Valley, I requested he take a picture of me sitting in the snow with our dog Grumpy,” she says. "I proposed the same way he proposed to me, the moment captured on my Rolleiflex.” Parker used diamonds and emeralds from her late great-grandmother’s engagement ring and gold from Karan’s late grandmother’s earrings to create his signet ring.

Their multi-day wedding unfolded in early September 2026 near Sun Valley, Idaho (where Parker’s family lives). They chose Central Idaho 4-H Camp, located 30 minutes outside of town, for its series of cabins and a large field up in the mountains. “The space was a blank canvas with no sound ordinance,” she says. “Studio Sully transformed the campsite into something magical.”

Going into the planning process, the couple knew they wanted the entire event to feel like a fusion of their cultures. In the days leading up to the Sun Valley celebration, Karan’s parents threw a series of traditional Indian wedding ceremonies and celebrations in his hometown of Pleasanton, California. Once guests arrived in Idaho, they all checked in at The Sun Valley Lodge, where each day the couple had organized group activities.

Festivities kicked off on Friday with a Western-themed welcome party at their favorite après-ski bar, Grumpy’s. Parker wore a tweed corset and skirt by Wiederhoeft accessorized with brown leather Manolo Blahniks and a wooden horse purse by Vin. When the weather cooled down, she tossed on a vintage suede fringe RRL jacket. Karan followed a similar script and wore a Bode suede fringe jacket and One of These Days pants with a painted horse and cowboy detail on the knees. His bandana was made by his mother. “She created a piece inspired by Marie Lichtenberg’s jeweled bandana, utilizing earrings and jewelry of Karan’s grandmothers, mother, and sister,” Parker says.

Guests were encouraged to wear textiles, paisley, and eclectic patterns to evoke a Wild West meets India atmosphere. In one corner, Boise Thread Studio set up shop to embroider initials onto trucker hats, shirts, and bandanas. Meanwhile, guests drank schooners (the name for Grumpy’s beer chalices) on the patio and ate burgers and hot dogs. “Karan and I named our beloved miniature dachshund Grumpy after the bar’s corn dogs, so it is only fitting that we ordered them until they ran out,” Parker says. Her mother welcomed the group with a sunset toast before moving inside. “We took over the electronic jukebox and closed down the restaurant until Karan had to stand up on the booth to usher everyone out the door.”

The next afternoon they hosted a sangeet by the Big Wood River. “The estate has a series of winding paths that lead to the riverside, and we wanted the afternoon portion to feel like a mountain garden party with activities and new discoveries tucked into each corner,” Parker says. The flowing river and live music by Boise-based Ashley Rose Band served as the soundtrack as guests played croquet, horseshoes, and badminton. Near the band, Henna by Sanjana created patterns on guests' hands.

Parker wore Danielle Frankel’s cream Mariela dress featuring two layers of hand-painted wildflowers. “I wanted a long braid with flowers woven in, and Jasmine Burnside helped bring the vision to life,” she says. Karan wore a light blue Jatin Malik sherwani with juttis from his personal collection.

As the sun set, guests were escorted to a clearing where a buffet was set up for dinner, and the dance floor was built around a large willow tree. The vignette was inspired by a 1905 Impressionist painting called Nighttime Festivities that depicts a turn-of-the-century party beneath the trees of a French palace. “I told Melissa [Sully] I wanted the sangeet to be based on this and we became obsessed with bringing the painting to life,” Parker explains. Lights and flowers were strung into the branches that draped overhead. Karan’s parents and Parker’s best friend, Ana, gave speeches before a series of traditional dances were performed by various family members and friends, followed by a lively dance floor courtesy of DJ Anupya.

The morning of the wedding, Parker and Karan got ready with their families and friends. “We are terrible at surprises, and he had seen my dress many times before, so I saw no purpose in a first look,” she says. The couple set off in a convertible (Parker’s hair protected in a vintage scarf from the year she was born), stopping at a vista for photos before arriving at the venue.

For her wedding look, Parker worked with Patricia Voto of One/Of to create a custom gown inspired by Christian Dior’s 1955 Soirée de Lahore. Early on, she knew she wanted something that felt like a combination of Karan and herself. “The original gown was made by the designer two years before his death and was based on a sari with the one-shoulder design and gold embroidery,” she explains. All of the embroidery was done in India, and Parker paired the gown with a custom One/Of veil featuring gold embroidery along the edge, as well as light green shoes by Paula Nadal.

Karan wore a custom Bode tuxedo with a golden sunflower detail, and as a boutonniere, he wore his own emerald kalgi (a traditional Indian headpiece for turbans). “His cufflinks and studs were my late grandfather’s, his cummerbund was unbranded vintage ’70s made in India with embellished sunflowers, and his shoes were suede Hermès.”

Guests arrived to the ceremony dressed in jewel tones and metallics, walking down a pathway with giant sunflowers on either side. Parker and her mother, an amateur florist, had collaborated with Madison of Hart Floral to create the dramatic entrance. “When I spoke of our love for sunflowers and our desire to have them serve as a recurring visual throughout the day, Madison and Melissa joined forces,” Parker explains.

For the ceremony, they stood within a chuppah structure with draped gold chainmail and burgundy sunflower motifs. Karan’s best friend Drew officiated the ceremony, and the fathers of her late friends Alex and Josh read an excerpt by Ovid of the Greek myth of Baucis and Philemon. “Karan’s vows and my own were mirrors of each other. Unknowingly, we made many of the same references, and we each ended by quoting the story of Baucis and Philemon, who, when they died, received the honor of becoming intertwined as trees so they could live on forever.”

Coming from mixed religious backgrounds (Karan’s parents are Sikh and Hindu and Parker’s are Episcopalian and Jewish), the couple chose to honor both. “Karan arrived on horseback as a nod to traditional Punjabi weddings. St. Christopher medallions were placed on each program, and my sister, Alison, shared a short speech about the patron saint of travelers, the namesake of the church we attended as children, and spoke about the new journey Karan and I were embarking upon,” Parker says. “My sister-in-law, Kareena, shared a speech about the jaimala ceremony as a nod to the Hindu wedding tradition, and spoke about the importance of seeing each other as equals.” Karan’s maternal uncle led the group in a Sikh Ardas prayer, and the ceremony ended with the Jewish tradition of the breaking of the glass. As the couple exited, guests threw dried bougainvillea from Karan’s family garden in Chandigarh.

Cocktail hour kicked off with oysters shipped from Island Creek Oyster Co. (the site of the couple’s first two dates). “We really wanted textiles to be an important decor motif throughout the celebration,” Parker says, noting that there was leveled seating overlaid with rugs, a rug-covered bar, and a rug-covered makeshift bandstand. Later on, their sisters shared speeches, and the couple danced to “Can’t Take My Eyes Off You,” then launching into “Dancing in the Moonlight” as tambourines were passed to guests. “Guests followed Karan and I through the field to reveal the tent, set among the cabins.”

The band continued to play as guests entered the tent and danced. At each seat, Parker and Karan placed a letter explaining what their friendship meant to the couple. After the buzz died down, Parker’s father opened the evening with a speech. “We then had a family-style dinner of our favorite bites: kale salad, focaccia, Idaho trout, pesto gnocchi, and vodka meatballs,” she says.

After dinner wrapped up, Parker and her father danced to “Wide Open Spaces” (a song they listened to as she was growing up) before everyone joined in. The couple snuck away to cut the cake—a 30-inch round flourless chocolate confection covered in raspberries—which was passed out on the outskirts of the dance floor. Indian sweets called ladoos were also passed around.

After a full week of wedding events between California for the traditional Indian celebration and four days in Sun Valley, the two were understandably exhausted. “It took weeks to recover, but the whole time all we could talk about was how deeply happy we were.”

#6776. Willy Chavarria Is Honored at the 6th Annual We Are All Human Gala

Plus, special recognition for Dolores Huerta and Mark D. Levine and a Cirque du Soleil performance.

Last night at Cipriani Wall Street, the sixth annual We Are All Human Gala celebrated the advancement of the Latin community. “Latinos are prosperity, power, and progress, but the stereotypes, the biases, and the negative narratives have not disappeared,” Claudia Romo Edelman, founder and executive chair of We Are All Human, said in her opening remarks. “That’s why this moment matters, because we have many Latino moments that we together can turn into momentum, and use that momentum to change our perception for good, once and for all.”

We Are All Human, through its Hispanic Star initiative, is a nonprofit aimed at uplifting Hispanics in economic and cultural spaces. According to its website, “Hispanic Star seeks to reframe Hispanics not merely as a fast-growing demographic, but as a vital economic, cultural, and civic force shaping the United States’ future.”

Throughout the evening, many leaders were recognized for their contributions to society. These included fashion designer Willy Chavarria, CEO and creative director of Willy Chavarria; Mark D. Levine, New York City comptroller; Bea Perez, executive vice president and global chief communications, sustainability, and strategic partnerships officer at The Coca-Cola Company, and chair of The Coca-Cola Foundation; and Henry R. Muñoz III, American activist and founder and CEO of Cultural Productions.

“For generations, our people have been told where we can go, how far we can rise, and which doors we’re allowed to walk through on our own land,” Chavarria said in his speech. “Now, it’s the time for us to change all of that. When one of us succeeds, we all get a little bit more powerful.”

Other individuals were also inducted into the organization’s CMO Hall of Fame, and various community organizers and tastemakers gave testimonials on We Are All Human’s impact in their lives. The late labor leader Dolores Huerta was also honored in a moving tribute, and Chavarria also mentioned the feminist activist in his speech. “She carried such strength and humanity and grace, and we all learned from her,” the designer said. “In this time when we see our brothers and sisters and our brown friends and family being treated like animals and abducted and sent away and put in camps and all of that, it’s just so important for us to stand tall in who we are right now.”

Cirque du Soleil took the stage to perform, and guests were given shots of Sotol Romo in champagne flutes for a toast. Co-chairs Gabriel de Alba, founder and managing partner of GDA Luma Capital Management; Karla Martínez de Salas, chief content officer of AVANCE; Bing Chen, CEO and co-founder of Gold House; and Marissa Solis, SVP of global brand and consumer marketing of the NFL, also attended the event. We Are All Human also surpassed its fundraising goals, with two individuals spending $32,000 on a Super Bowl package during the live auction.

“The We Are All Human Foundation is equipped to turn this momentum into results, through data and insights, to guide decisions and to inspire others,” Romo Edelman told the crowd. “Our co-chairs and honorees are the pure embodiment of the Latino brand that we want the world to see.”

Samuel is the Content Strategy Manager at Hearst. Prior to this role, he was an Associate Editor and the Assistant to ELLE’s Editor-in-Chief, Nina Garcia. Raised in Des Moines, Iowa, Samuel attended Northwestern University and currently resides in New York City. He is probably humming a tune at his desk right now.

#6772. Why Men Are Posting Thirst Traps in Red Light Face Masks

Gonzalo Fernández Escribano knew all about the benefits of red-light therapy for his skin. Fewer wrinkles, a brighter complexion, minimizing acne—he’d experienced it all firsthand when he tried out LED masks during his facials. In March 2025, he purchased one for at-home use. Little did he know it would come with an extra benefit as a provocative prop for a sexy photo.

"I use my red-light mask right before bed, so I’m already shirtless,” says Escribano. “Let’s just say the sexy-looking picture is easily convenient to take.”

Escribano is one of many men online who’ve embraced the red-light thirst trap. More and more, image-conscious guys with followings built around some combination of beauty, fitness, wellness, travel, are pairing their red-light routines with the occasional shirtless selfie. The trend is especially prevalent in gay-male internet culture, where faceless torsos shots, sometimes featuring beefy chests and six-pack abs, have been part of the visual language of dating apps and social media for years. A shirtless red-light-mask pic is a variation on the theme: The body and the anonymity are still there—only now there’s a skincare routine attached.

Jayson Stacy, a celebrity groomer, noticed his friend wearing an LED in his Instagram profile picture three years ago. At the same time, Stacy had recently gotten a mask himself and decided to follow suit, posting a photo of his own after Mardi Gras under the guise of some much needed recovery.

“It’s almost like a sexy Halloween costume, but you don’t have to wait until October,” Stacy says. Escribano agrees, saying it makes him look like a superhero. Strong shadows, glowing skin, and a Sith-like red glow mix together in a thirsty trifecta meant to slow someone’s scrolling.

For Stacy, red-light masks strike the perfect balance between wellness and plausible deniability. “You’re supposed to use the mask after you wash your face, so most are fresh out of the shower before using,” he says. “It’s like, ‘Oh, look at my morning routine,’ which gives the perfect excuse to be mostly unclothed. Then you have the mask on, you feel like someone can’t see your face, so it can feel like a layer of protection.” In other words, the skin care routine becomes the reason for the thirst trap, even though it’s only half the motivation.

Red-light therapy is a noninvasive treatment that uses low-level LED light to stimulate cellular activity and boost collagen production. The technology has become especially prominent over the last few years thanks to its ability to reduce inflammation, soften fine lines, and promote skin and muscle recovery and hair growth. (That is, as long as you are willing to invest in high-quality equipment and use it every single day).

Celebrities have been documenting their LED routines for years, from January Jones to Kim Kardashian. But those posts were mostly framed around the masks’ intended purpose: improving your skin. Somewhere along the way, though, a certain breed of extremely online men—trainers, models, artists, content creators, and guys who simply know their angles—found a second purpose for the masks: as an accessory for a hot photo. The masks are turning up in bathroom mirror pics, bedroom shots, and buried several slides deep in otherwise unrelated photo dumps, often without any mention of skin care at all. And judging by the comments—fire emojis, heart eyes, and the occasional “yummy”—their followers seem to be noticing something other than the mask.

The thirst traps are also surfacing on social media at a time when curiosity around red-light therapy is booming. A 2025 study analyzing Google Trends data found that search interest across red-light-therapy-related terms increased 118% after February 2024. And by 2025, Google reported that searches for “red light therapy” had reached an all-time high. As these futuristic devices have rapidly flooded our feeds, maybe it was only a matter of time before they made their way out of the skin care selfie and into something a little thirstier.

In some ways these images are being ushered into well-trodden territory. In 2021, TikTok’s viral Silhouette Challenge had users transform themselves into barely distinguishable, body-forward silhouettes under a red filter, turning the color itself into a visual shorthand for sex appeal. Meanwhile, for gay men, shirtless photos are hardly a novelty, especially on dating apps like Grindr and Sniffies, where headless torsos are practically part of the experience. “The headless torsos definitely all blend together at this point,” says John Arthur Hill, who compares swiping through hookup apps to looking into “a lukewarm batch of Chili’s skillet queso” and trying to figure out what’s cheese, meat, or a bell pepper.

And even when the most impressive six-pack can get lost in an endless doom scroll, red light can add just enough intrigue to get someone to look twice. For Adam Pent, an OnlyFans creator who’s no stranger to posting thirst traps himself, that curiosity is part of the appeal. “It [a red-light-mask thirst trap] is definitely more interesting because I’d be curious about who’s behind the mask,” he says. To Pent, there’s something creative about the whole thing. “I guess it makes the photo a bit more artful too? ‘Art thot’ vibes I guess, unless it looks like sponsored content.” It also makes him want to shop. “I’m less focused on the body and more like, ‘Should I just get one already?’”

The red-light thirst trap also holds a certain mysterious appeal. “I’ve been posting ‘masked’ selfies for quite some time because I think it’s a really hot aesthetic and such a cool fantasy,” Michael Anthony, a celebrity makeup artist, says. For him, it’s not just about looking good in the photos. “The fantasy of anonymity and pleasure, while doing something for yourself that will make you look and feel better—it’s all so sexy.”

An LED face mask is a luxurious way to get an at-home glow, and Shark's is one of our favorites. It helps to fight acne and fine lines, and it can help depuff around the eyes for those long nights.

Tattoo artist Alvin Archibald sees a similar appeal. “There’s a subtle tension between vulnerability and control that makes it interesting,” he says. “The mask takes away your facial expressions, so the body becomes the focus. It creates this interesting dynamic where you’re both exposed and concealed at the same time. There’s something almost intimate about it, like you’re getting ready for an audience without necessarily admitting it.”

#6771. Inside the Confetti-Filled Opening Night of ‘Much Ado About Nothing,’ Starring Tom Hiddleston and Hayley Atwell

Forget the traditional opening-night fanfare. If love is a many-splendored thing, apparently it’s also bright pink and best enjoyed with a ’90s dance anthem blasting in the background.

William Shakespeare’s Much Ado About Nothing arrived at the Winter Garden Theatre on Thursday with an explosion of fuchsia confetti, a giant inflatable heart, and seriously impressive dance moves from Tom Hiddleston and Hayley Atwell. Shakespeare, it seemed, was ready to party.

Following its successful run in London’s West End, the production crossed the Atlantic with Hiddleston and Atwell reprising their roles as Benedick and Beatrice, two sharp-witted characters who trade barbs before falling in love. At the show’s helm is acclaimed British director Jamie Lloyd, known for his bold, contemporary reimaginings of classic plays and musicals. With Much Ado About Nothing, he’s given Shakespeare’s romantic comedy a gloriously over-the-top makeover.

The party started long before the curtain rose. From the moment guests stepped into the Winter Garden, ushers danced down the aisles, greeting theatergoers as though they were arriving at the hottest club. Cynthia Nixon, Rachel Zegler, Bryan Cranston, Claire Danes, Angela Bassett, Bob Odenkirk, Hugh Dancy, and Busy Philipps were among the familiar faces in the crowd, which had enough star power to rival the theatrical fireworks unfolding onstage.

As Benedick and Beatrice, Hiddleston, best known to many as Marvel’s Loki, and Atwell, who played Agent Peggy Carter in the MCU, made an irresistibly mischievous pairing—dancing, singing, and flirting with infectious enthusiasm. Their characters’ courtship is one of literature’s great enemies-to-lovers stories, and the pair brought every barb, raised eyebrow, and moment of romantic tension brilliantly to life.

“I can’t do much, but I can lunge,” Hiddleston modestly told Vogue of his dancing before curtain. “The dancing is sufficiently irreverent and giddy and silly and free as this play, and I’m having the best time.”

Dressed in a Ralph Lauren tuxedo, he described his opening-night feelings as “joyous and joyful,” declaring his love for the play, his fellow cast members, and the production. It was during his previous collaboration with Lloyd that the seeds for this one were planted. “When I was last on Broadway, for the production of Betrayal, Jamie said to me on the last night, ‘What are we doing next?’ I thought he was joking and I said, ‘Much Ado About Nothing,’ and here we are!”

For Atwell, the evening marked a major milestone in her nearly 25-year acting career: she was making her Broadway debut. Clad in a chic Erdem ensemble, she reflected on the significance of the occasion shortly before taking the stage. “It’s really remarkable. It’s not lost on me how special this moment is,” she said. “Rather than being overwhelmed by it, I really want to take it in and to cherish it.” She described herself as staying “calm amongst the excitable storm of it all.”

Atwell was also having a blast with the production’s choreography, giving audiences a chance to see a different side of her. “I don’t have any formal dance training apart from going to the clubs,” she said, laughing. “I get to have a party every night onstage, and make moves I didn’t know I had. So it’s really fun.” The dancing came with a few occupational hazards, however, including sore knees and the occasional spill. “I’ve already slipped on my ass twice, and that felt like an initiation,” she shared. Rather than rattling her, the falls actually made her more confident: “I can survive this. I can get up and carry on.”

For all the fuchsia and dance-floor theatrics, the production left the Bard’s text alone. “I’ve always believed that Shakespeare should be spoken as if it was written right now or yesterday,” Hiddleston said. “There’s no word of this play that isn’t the 400-year-old text by Shakespeare. We have dressed it in the energy of dancing and music and levity, but the play itself is the play that it always was.”

He went on: “Come because it will speak to your heart. This play is funny. It’s been funny for 400 years. We’ll make it easy for you.”

Dressed in a custom pink Levi’s suit, Lloyd told Vogue that the show’s abundance of confetti—which erupts during a jubilant party scene, blanketing the stage and eventually spilling into the audience—is about more than just spectacle. “It’s certainly a lot and it’s used in a really exciting way, to show the celebration of love and joy—and that’s what the play is all about. It feels like we really need that right now.”

The party continued well after the curtain came down. Following a standing ovation, the cast and guests headed to Tavern on the Green for the after-party, where lavish food stations served tacos, pasta, salmon, and steak skewers, while the Prosecco flowed—and, yes, people hit the dance floor.

#6768. Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Quentin Jones is taking her surrealist art off the wall and onto the body with “Nocturne,” her first jewelry collection; Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reinterprets the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up”; and Kendra Scott launches the new “Kendra Scott Collection,” reimagining her signature Spike motif as a personal talisman. From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back. For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Quentin Jones is taking her art off the wall and onto the body with “Nocturne,” the first jewelry collection from House of Quentin Jones. Following the multidisciplinary artist’s debut of surrealist ceramics and tapestries last year, the new collection transforms recurring gestures and symbols from her work into sculptural collars, cuffs, armbands, pendants, chains, and earrings. Eyes, hands, stars, moons, tears, and knots take shape in silver and gold vermeil alongside freshwater pearls, onyx, opals, jade, glass, and silk tassels, giving the pieces a dreamlike, talismanic quality. Beginning as drawings and hand collages in Jones’s Brooklyn studio, each design carries her surrealist visual language into wearable form, further blurring the line between art, design, and fashion.

Piotrek Panszczyk Makes His Debut at Ippolita

Ippolita is entering a new chapter under creative director Piotrek Panszczyk, who makes his debut at the house with “Ancient Bohemia,” a collection inspired by its archives and the organic forms of nature. Taking a sculptural approach to jewelry, Panszczyk reimagines familiar signatures with an emphasis on texture, craftsmanship, and unconventional layering. The house’s iconic Wavy Disk becomes the Wavy Coin, featuring Hippolyta, Queen of the Amazons and the brand’s namesake, as a symbol of feminine strength. Elsewhere, hand-sculpted Stardust pavé is set entirely with diamonds, pearls make their first appearance, and lightweight, hand-hammered silver Roma links offer multiple ways to wear, from necklaces to belts. Lapis lazuli, mother-of-pearl, and quartz introduce color through Ippolita’s signature layered doublets and triplets, mixed rather than matched for an eclectic finish. The result is a fresh interpretation of the house’s heritage that feels both ancient and distinctly modern.

Jade Trau and Luis Morais Combine Gemstones and Diamonds

Jade Trau and Luis Morais are joining forces for a limited-edition collection that brings together their different approaches to fine jewelry. Comprised of 16 one-of-a-kind pieces, including 12 bracelets and four necklaces, the collaboration pairs Morais’s colorful gemstone beads with Trau’s signature 18-karat gold and diamond elements. The designs feature a vibrant mix of stones, from chrysoprase and amazonite to lapis lazuli, turquoise, carnelian, and tiger’s-eye, accented by recognizable Jade Trau details like the Frankie rondelle, Sophisticate station, and Georgie diamond lock. Designed with everyday wear in mind, the bracelets lend themselves to layering, while the necklaces incorporate Trau’s diamond-set closures. The result is a playful meeting of color and craftsmanship that brings a fresh perspective to both designers’ signatures.

Lagos Looks to the Stars With Its Latest Collection

Lagos references the night sky in its latest fine jewelry collection, taking inspiration from the North Star and its centuries-old symbolism of guidance and possibility. Designed to celebrate milestones, new beginnings, and meaningful connections, the “North Star” collection features sculptural star motifs crafted in 18-karat gold and illuminated by natural diamonds. Cascading silhouettes and light-catching details bring a sense of movement to the celestial designs, balancing statement-making shapes with delicate craftsmanship. Both symbolic and sophisticated, the assortment offers a modern interpretation of a timeless emblem, making each piece a reminder of the people and moments that guide us.

Kendra Scott Launches an Eponymous Collection

Kendra Scott is offering a more personal look into her design world with the launch of the “Kendra Scott Collection,” a new line spanning fashion and fine jewelry. Its first edition, “The Spike,” revisits a silhouette the designer has worn throughout her career as a personal symbol of strength, confidence, and conviction. Inspired by nature and the radiance of the night sky, the collection translates the pointed motif into sculptural necklaces, earrings, bracelets, and charms, balancing bold proportions with intricate craftsmanship. Fashion jewelry incorporates tiger’s eye, malachite, and turquoise, while fine jewelry introduces lab-grown diamonds, pavé detailing, labradorite, and lapis. From statement-making links to delicate diamond charms, the collection offers a new interpretation of Scott’s longtime signature, inviting wearers to make the symbol their own.

Alison Lou’s ‘New Wave’ Collection Experiments With New Materials

Alison Lou is entering a new chapter with “New Wave,” a fine jewelry collection that introduces unexpected materials and a fresh approach to the designer’s signature playful aesthetic. Exploring the relationship between movement and form, the collection pairs lightweight titanium and ceramic with lab-grown diamonds, creating fluid, sculptural silhouettes that curve and catch the light. Available in Fig and Denim Blue colorways, the designs include wavy rings, diamond-accented signets, delicate thread necklaces, and statement drop earrings. With its unconventional materials and easy-to-wear designs, “New Wave” brings a new sense of movement to Alison Lou’s everyday jewelry.

Jenny Bird and Emese Gormley Partner on the Anyu Ring

Jenny Bird is expanding her JB FINE collection with a new collaboration featuring lifestyle creator and Lipstick on the Rim podcast host Emese Gormley. Named in honor of Gormley’s mother, the Anyu Ring offers a personal take on the classic signet, featuring three lab-grown baguette diamonds set in 14-karat yellow or white gold. The understated design combines the timeless appeal of a signet ring with a sentimental tribute to family, bringing a new perspective to Bird’s growing fine jewelry offering. Available exclusively on Jenny Bird’s website, the collaboration makes a case for jewelry that feels as meaningful as it looks.

Pomellato Revives Its 1997 Moneta Orientale Pendant as a Colorful Talisman

Pomellato is looking to its archives for its latest good-luck charm. For the newest chapter of “Memorie d’Archivio,” the maison revives Moneta Orientale, a 1997 pendant inspired by the ancient symbolism of a circle surrounding a square, representing the harmony between heaven and earth. Made in 33 stone-and-pavé combinations, the design pairs colorful hard stones including black jade, amazonite, sodalite, rhodochrosite, and labradorite with Pomellato’s signature irregular pavé of diamonds, sapphires, rubies, and more. Offered in two proportions, each pendant transforms the archival motif into a modern talisman for prosperity and protection.

Bibi van der Velden Makes Her High Jewelry Debut With ‘Eclipse’

Bibi van der Velden is entering a new era with “Eclipse,” the designer’s first high jewelry collection and most refined exploration of diamonds to date. Inspired by the fleeting moment when light gives way to darkness, the collection includes two six-piece suites: one in white diamonds and another defined by an ombré of diamonds into gray spinels deepening into black. Van der Velden’s signature alligator motif is abstracted into fluid, sculptural silhouettes, with scales engraved into the white gold on the reverse of each piece. “With Eclipse, I wanted to take those familiar elements and transform them into something more abstract and sculptural,” she says. “It’s a collection about transformation, which has always been at the heart of my work.”

Aflalo and Leandra Medine Cohen Turn Everyday Indulgences Into Fine Jewelry

Aflalo is finding the humor in our obsession with wellness with “The Vanities,” a new two-piece fine jewelry capsule designed exclusively by Leandra Medine Cohen. Playing with the contradiction between longevity and life’s little indulgences, the collection transforms two unlikely everyday accessories into precious objects. The Memento Mori is a 14-karat yellow gold cigar band set with more than four carats of diamonds, designed to slip over an Oura Ring, while The Libertine is a sterling silver pendant case adorned with diamonds and a wild-haired woman outlined in gold, designed to hold nicotine pouches or other small essentials. Equal parts extravagant and irreverent, the collection makes a playful case for enjoying life’s pleasures without sacrificing personal style.

Julia Berolzheimer’s Hart Jewelry Collection Draws on Childhood Memories

Hart is teaming up with Julia Berolzheimer for a new jewelry collection that celebrates the art of the stack. Inspired by Berolzheimer’s childhood memories of her grandmother’s California ranch and her collection of Art Deco minaudières, the collaboration brings together whimsical charms, colorful gemstones, and meaningful talismans designed to be mixed, layered, and restyled. Botanical motifs take shape in Tree of Life coins, daisies, bees, and fruit-inspired charms, while carnelian, turquoise, and pink opal add playful pops of color. One standout is the Perfume Vesta Case, a modern interpretation of a Victorian-era accessory that can hold a sample-sized fragrance and hang alongside other charms. With interchangeable chains, cords, and pendants, the collection makes a case for jewelry that feels just as personal as the memories behind it.

David Yurman Captures the Magic of a New York Holiday

David Yurman is celebrating the magic of the holidays in New York City with a new campaign featuring global brand ambassador Michael B. Jordan alongside actresses Yerin Ha and Inde Navarrette. Set against a snow-covered Washington Square Park, the campaign captures a series of quintessential New York moments, from Jordan and Ha facing off in a spirited card game to Navarrette hailing a cab in search of the perfect holiday gift. Throughout the campaign, the cast wears some of the house’s most recognizable designs, including 18-karat gold Cable bracelets and pieces from the “Starburst” and “Armory” collections. Directed by Bradford Young and photographed by Drew Vickers, the campaign brings together the spirit of the city, the joy of togetherness, and the timeless appeal of David Yurman’s jewelry.

Vhernier Reintroduces Its Iconic ‘Trottola’ Design With New Proportions

Vhernier is revisiting one of its most recognizable designs with a new interpretation of “Trottola,” the sculptural collection originally introduced in 2003. Inspired by the continuous motion of a spinning top, the latest designs play with proportions, contrasting materials, and asymmetrical shapes across rings, bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Polished rose gold meets glossy black jet, with select pieces featuring diamond pavé for an added contrast of light and dark. The collection also focuses on versatility, from rings designed for stacking to convertible earclips that can be worn as studs or dangling earrings. With its fluid forms and unexpected material combinations, “Trottola” brings a fresh perspective to Vhernier’s signature sculptural approach to jewelry.

Di Volpe is rethinking the classic tennis bracelet with “DiSegno,” a new customizable fine jewelry system designed to change with its wearer. Using concealed, patent-pending connections, individual cultured diamonds and gemstones, called Elements, can be removed, rearranged, or added by hand, allowing wearers to transform bracelets into necklaces or earrings without compromising the seamless look of traditional tennis jewelry. The collection launches with 28 stone-and-cut combinations, including rubies, emeralds, sapphires, and diamonds, alongside a limited-edition collaboration with Lebanese artist Ali Cha’aban featuring five sculptural designs. With new cuts, colors, and artist collaborations planned for future releases, “DiSegno” makes the case for fine jewelry that’s never quite finished.

Brent Neale Riffs on the Bow in Fine Jewelry

Brent Neale is putting a bow on the holiday season with “Wrap It Up,” a new fine jewelry collection that transforms the familiar ribbon motif into sculptural works of art. Drawing inspiration from Old Hollywood glamour and the femininity of the 1960s, the designer plays with the contrast between rigid precious metals and the soft movement of fabric, crafting bows that twist, curl, and unfurl in 18-karat gold and platinum. Colored gemstones sit nestled within the ribbon-like forms, while pavé diamonds accentuate every curve and fold. Equal parts playful and precious, the collection brings a little gift-wrapped glamour to the jewelry box.

Grace Winfrey’s ‘Poppy Fortuna’ Collection Reimagines Good-Luck Charms

Grace Winfrey is bringing a little extra luck to the jewelry box with “Poppy Fortuna,” a new fine jewelry collection inspired by the natural beauty of the Italian landscape and symbols of good fortune. Scarlet poppies, sunflowers, bees, four-leaf clovers, horseshoes, and ladybugs come to life through vibrant gemstones and intricate pavé, creating miniature mosaics of color and texture. Highlights include a Lucky Bangle combining three good-luck charms, sculptural sunflower earrings with tiny ladybugs tucked into their petals, and layered charm necklaces designed to feel like modern keepsakes. Handcrafted in New York City, each piece transforms familiar symbols of prosperity, protection, and happiness into playful, colorful works of art.

Robinson Pelham Channels ’90s Glamour for Its Anniversary

Robinson Pelham is closing out its 30th anniversary celebrations with “Suite 96,” a new campaign and collection launch inspired by the glamour of late-’90s London. Set inside a private suite at St. James’s Hotel & Club in Mayfair, the campaign considers jewelry the ultimate room service, with diamonds arriving on trays, gold spilling out of wardrobes, and plenty of pieces to layer before a night out. At the center is “Studio 30,” a four-piece anniversary collection featuring diamond necklaces and statement earrings, including the sculptural Via and Hira necklaces, cascading diamond drops, and the bold Solstice Supreme. Alongside the anniversary designs, the house introduces new collections and additions to existing favorites, from chunky gold links and graphic heart rings to colorful enamel and gemstone combinations. Together, the launches celebrate three decades of Robinson Pelham’s bold, playful approach to fine jewelry.

Le Vian and Jared Revive Georgian-Era Romance

Le Vian is bringing a little old-world romance to Jared Jewelers with its new exclusive “Georgian Chocolate & Nude Diamonds” collection. Inspired by the candlelit jewels of the Georgian era, when jewelry was designed to capture and reflect the warmth of natural light, the collection rethinks historic buttercup settings in polished Honey Gold, with eight rounded prongs framing each natural diamond. The designs pair Le Vian’s signature Chocolate and Nude Diamonds with warm gold detailing across solitaire rings, anniversary bands, tennis bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Select styles introduce colorful gemstones including Chocolate Quartz, Green Apple Peridot, and Grape Amethyst, giving the vintage-inspired silhouettes a contemporary update. With its sculptural settings and warm-toned stones, the collection brings the romance of antique jewelry into the present.

Wempe’s ‘Au:thentic’ Celebrates the Art of Goldsmithing

Wempe’s new gender-inclusive fine jewelry collection, “Au:thentic,” puts craftsmanship front and center, finding inspiration in the foundational skills taught to aspiring goldsmiths. The collection takes its shape from the jump ring, one of the first techniques Patrick Sieroslawski learned as an apprentice more than 22 years ago, translating its form to necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings, and pendants. Handcrafted at Wempe’s atelier in Germany, the pieces come in 18-karat yellow, white, and rose gold, with select styles set with diamonds. “Au:thentic” also marks the beginning of a multiyear rollout, with three additional chapters set to expand on its design language through twists, wraps, and interlocking links.

#6767. APC Just Dropped a Pair of Incredibly Clean Casios

We’re in a great era for affordable fashion-collab watches. Timex is leading the way: its link-ups with Noah, Todd Snyder, and J. Crew are masterclasses in adding personality to classic designs without going OTT. But don’t count Casio out of the race. The Japanese giant has just followed up its 2024 collab with APC. The pair are perfectly matched: Casio watches are known for being beautifully functional and understated—adjectives that fit APC’s spartan aesthetic too.

The first collaboration between the two brands saw them rework the hallowed A1000 digital-display watch. That release stripped back a lot of the original’s markings and added the APC logo on the dial. This time around, it's the AQ-240 getting a makeover. The case is squarer than the rectangular A1000, and while there’s a narrow window for a digital display, the watch maintains the traditional analog format with hands and hour markers. Here, you get the best of both worlds. The watch is sold out on APC's website but still available through Casio, so act fast if you want one. The silver variant is $110, and the gold goes for $135.

What about the APC-ness of it all? That part is the opposite of loud: as with the previous collab, there isn’t much giving away the brand's participation. We’ve got the APC logo on the dial and the buckle, plus the brand’s guitar-and-dagger insignia engraved out of sight on the caseback. Casio already offers the regular A1000 in a bunch of different colorways, but these two new ones are made specifically for the APC collab. The release includes a silver case with a dark-green dial and another in gold on gold. Both of the pieces have a brushed sun-ray effect on the dial. The vibe is refined and stripped back; this is a fashion watch that almost feels anti-fashion.

Maybe the best way to think about it is as the reverse side of the coin to the excellent new G-Shock 2500 Series. Both come from Casio, and both are minimal and functional. But the G-Shocks are big and bulky, with a design that seems to tip its hat to Patek Philippe’s Nautilus. These new ones are slim and nearly dressy. Put the two new releases together, and you’ve got pretty much all bases covered. And if those aforementioned Timex collabs felt a bit too classicist for you, then the Casio-APC partnership takes things in a more modern direction. Far from just being a cash grab, the “fashion watch” is now one of the best routes into affordable, on-point horology.

#6765. Do You Always Have to Sacrifice Passion for Emotional Intimacy?

Do you remember the beginning of your relationship with your partner? When you would be turned on by just the thought of your fingers sharing space with theirs? When you’d bust into the apartment movie-style, tossing aside layers of clothes because you just couldn’t wait to get to skin?

Now, your relationship looks a little different. There are no mental gymnastics before grabbing your partner’s hand and you don’t mind putting your clothes away nicely before jumping into bed. It’s a comfortable, cozy phase—one that you worked hard and long to get to.

But sometimes, you might wish you could have both.

We’ve collectively accepted that a long-term relationship somehow means the end of passion. You spend months or even years learning everything you can about your partner. You’ve gained trust, a strong foundation, and yet, the passion seems to have disappeared with the uncertainty.

“The comfort you worked so hard to build may be what’s subtly killing your desire for your partner,” certified sex therapist Dr. Mindy DeSeta, PhD tells Cosmopolitan.

She explains that over time, we unknowingly become complacent about romancing our partner. All of the exciting unknowns of the beginning of a relationship (Do they want to kiss you? Do they want to come home with you?) eventually fade away.

“You may be intimately familiar with your partner's moves after a number of years, and that's a beautiful thing,” Dr. DeSeta says. “But repetition can dull the excitement of physical intimacy.”

But contrary to what we’ve all learned to accept, this isn’t an inevitable diagnosis for your long-term relationship. It is possible to have both emotional intimacy and sexual passion. You just have to work for it.

“Your sex life will suffer if you do not actively continue to learn your partner’s desires,” Dr. DeSeta says.

You might think that you know everything there is to know already about your partner. But as we get older, our desires change—even if our partner doesn’t.

“Your pleasure is constantly evolving and if you want to continue to have a thriving and exciting sex life, a couple must be able to talk about most recent desires,” she says. “What turned you on at 25 may not still turn you on at 40.”

This is why communicating your desires and feelings to your partner is so important. Sure, you and your partner may be pros at working through disagreements and emotionally supporting each other through life’s changes—but does that energy translate into your sex life? Do you share your curiosities? Do you hold space for them telling you they’d like to try something different? If you don’t, it might be time to make a change.

First, in order to feel sexy with your partner, you need to feel sexy yourself. Do something every day that makes you feel sexy and confident,” Dr. DeSeta says. “The foundation of a great sex life starts with each person having a thriving libido.”

Then try to initiate sexy conversation with your partner. Dr. DeSeta suggests planning something that feels tantalizing without the expectation of physical intimacy to follow. Reach each other erotica, talk about new fantasies you’ve had, discuss a trendy sex topic in media.

“Many long-term couples need a prompt to get the sexy conversations started,” she says. They also need reminders to touch each other more.

“Long term couples are notorious for forgetting physical affection,” she says. Dr. DeSeta emphasizes small touches every day. This can look like caressing their back as you pass them in the kitchen or holding hands while you watch your favorite show on the couch. Instead of maneuvering around each other, lean into the touch. It signals to your partner that you see them and want them throughout the day. And just the feeling of being wanted can ignite some of that passion from the early days of your relationship.

Most importantly, you should date your partner every day. Dr. DeSeta recommends “Daily Dates” to couples she sees in therapy. This is when you set aside 20 minutes everyday to make your partner a priority.

“All too often long-term couples put their relationship second, so this tune up is always needed,” she says. “During these 20 minutes the phones are silenced, logistical conversations are off the table, and the time is dedicated to emotionally connecting with their partner.”

By doing this, she explains that you slow down the daily hustle and really learn how to reconnect with your partner. You might even learn a few new things about them in the process.

“If we do not make the intentional effort to keep the passion alive, then you are at risk of falling into autopilot,” Dr.Deseta says. We often think passion and emotional intimacy are at odds with each other, but that’s because we think it’s acceptable to simply fall into our relationship without making an active effort to keep our relationship exciting. And no, you don’t have to be constantly ripping each other’s clothes off or feel nervous in order to have a passionate relationship. But you should aim to feel the same deep desire and attraction to your partner that you did when you first started off—if not more.

#6764. Holiday Travel With Babies and Pets Just Got a Whole Lot Easier

Smart travel essentials for the whole family—four-legged members included.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Seasonal travel is exciting, but getting everyone from point A to point B can be a production—especially when babies and pets are involved. Between navigating crowded airports, packing the car with what feels like half your house, and keeping your four-legged family members calm amid all the chaos, there’s a ton to think about. And while you can’t control flight delays, holiday traffic, or the timing of a much-needed diaper change, having the right travel gear can make a world of difference.

Enter Nuna and Tavo, two brands that bring thoughtful design, innovative safety features, and a beautiful aesthetic to (often hectic) family travel. Nuna’s premium baby gear helps take some of the stress out of everything from airport security to car-seat installations, while Tavo focuses the same level of attention on the safety and comfort of travel essentials designed specifically for pets. Think lightweight strollers that fold with one hand (because your other one is probably occupied), car seats that don’t require an online tutorial to install, and comfortable carriers that give your furry companions a safe spot of their own.

Whether you’re flying across the country to visit the grandparents, packing up for a holiday road trip, or exploring a new destination together, these six Nuna and Tavo essentials, available at Bloomingdale’s, can help make the journey a little more joyful for everyone involved.

Given crowded security lines, blowouts at the worst possible moment, and the sheer amount of stuff you have to tote, a lightweight stroller is a genuine lifesaver at the airport. At just 16.3 pounds, the Nuna VIAA cabn is designed to meet IATA cabin-size standards when folded with the armbar removed—though you’ll still want to double-check your airline’s requirements. One-handed steering makes navigating crowded terminals easier (especially when you’re juggling a much-needed iced coffee), while a roomy storage basket holds up to 22 pounds of diaper bags, coats, backup outfits, bottles, snacks, and approximately 47 things to keep your baby entertained. It even comes with a carrier bag for when it’s time to board.

This Nuna duo pairs the TRIV lx stroller, which folds with one hand and stands on its own, with the ultralight PIPA urbn infant car seat. And if you’ve ever broken a sweat or found yourself YouTubing car seat installation instructions for the 100th time, you’ll appreciate that this one clicks into place without a base, using its built-in pipaFIX rigid latch installation. Large rear wheels and all-wheel suspension pair with a spacious storage basket and handy tracking-device pocket to make the stroller ready for everyday outings.

The Nuna SWIV’s standout feature is button-activated 360-degree rotation on all four wheels, allowing it to move sideways and pivot through tight spaces without the struggle of squeezing through crowded airport lines and store aisles that clearly weren’t designed with strollers in mind. A one-handed recline with three positions, including a near-flat option, makes adjustments easy, and the stroller is travel-system-ready with any Nuna PIPA series infant car seat, allowing you to move baby from the car to the stroller.

The Tavo Crispin gives your pet a roomy, comfortable spot of their own, with mesh panels for ventilation and visibility so they can still keep eyes on their humans. It’s impact-tested and secures to your vehicle using lower anchors and a top tether, with a protective memory-foam guard for added peace of mind. And when the holiday travels are over, it collapses for easy storage without taking up precious space at home.

This Tavo travel system might be your new favorite on-the-road companion. The Maeve car seat attaches to the Roscoe Trek stroller frame, so you can go from the car to a festive outing without having to transfer your pup to a different carrier. All-terrain rear wheels and suspension make bumpy sidewalks easier to navigate, while one-touch brakes and a compact stroller fold come in handy when it’s time for a coffee stop or to load everything back into the car.

There’s a very particular type of heart-rate spike that comes with flying with a pet. You have to stand in the airline’s special-assistance line with your (probably nervous) dog or cat, only to finally reach the counter and hold your breath as the agent (and sometimes a supervisor) decides whether their carrier fulfills the rules. The Tavo Dupree II has a chic carry-on design that’s also crash-tested, complete with built-in vehicle connectors and a protective memory-foam guard. Mesh panels keep air circulating, while a trolley sleeve lets you slip it over your suitcase handle instead of carrying everything separately. You’ll still want to double-check your airline’s requirements before flying.
